_source line 1045 · 2026-05-21T21:34:35.074Z_

逐点评估：

---

**Q1: Gemini 95 题 interim 翻转风险**

当前：SICA=82/95, SC=83/95, Δ=-1/95=-1.05pp。全量 204 题 Δ 翻转为正（>0pp）需要：剩余 109 题中 SICA 比 SC 多对 **≥2 题**。

风险评估：**中低**。理由：
- 若剩余 109 题的真实 Δ 与 interim 一致（-1.05pp），全量 Δ ≈ -1.03pp，不翻转
- FOLIO 不按难度排序（我此前已核实 folio_0–folio_94 是顺序 ID），系统性偏差低
- 但 n=95 的 Δ 置信区间很宽（±数 pp），纯统计上翻转概率约 20-30%

**最坏情况对论文的影响**：即使 Gemini 全量 Δ 翻为正（如 +0.5pp），Spearman ρ 从 -1.0 降为 -0.9（n=5），p 从 0.0083 升至 ~0.042（仍显著）。monotonic 变成 "near-monotonic with one deviation"。**不致命，但叙事力度下降**。建议论文加注脚：*"Gemini results based on 95/204 problems (interim); full results pending."*

---

**Q2: 70B 数据缺失影响**

**影响较小**。论文已覆盖 7B (Mistral), 8B (LLaMA, R1-Distill), 14B (Qwen), 32B (QwQ), 以及 4 个 frontier 模型。70B (Qwen-72B AWQ) 填补的是 14B–frontier 之间的 scale gap。

Reviewer 是否追问：**可能性低**。scale coverage 从 7B 到 frontier 已经足够。QwQ-32B（C0<5%, SICA≡SC）已经证明 32B 级别也有 SCB。更关键的缺失是方法在 14B+ 模型上的验证（论文已 explicitly acknowledge 为 limitation），而不是一个额外 scale point。

如果提交时没有 70B 数据，可在 Limitations 提一句即可，不影响 story。

---

**Q3: Cross-SICA 2×2 整合建议**

Qwen3↔Mistral 双向 ProofWriter 数据支持的 claim：
- **Claim 7 (SCB universality)**：第二个 dataset 上的 BR>1 证据
- **Quality Ladder (L1≈L0)**：open-source 跨模型 extraction 在 ProofWriter 上是否也 ≈ same-model

**是否值得 V16 Patch**：取决于数据完整性。我刚报告了 `extraction_qwen3_same` 被意外清空的问题（170 文件在 backup 中）。如果 Director 选择恢复 backup 文件，2×2 数据可在数小时内完整。此时值得作为 **Appendix 补充表**（ProofWriter cross-model results），但不建议重构主文 — 时间风险太大。

如果 2×2 无法在 May 24 前完成，直接跳过。论文已有 5 个模型的 FOLIO cross-model 数据，足够支撑核心 claim。

---

**Q4: V16 最可能被 reviewer 攻击的 3 个点**

**Attack 1 (最高风险): Process-κ gradient 只有 n=5 个点**
即使 ρ=-1.0, p=0.0083，reviewer 会说 "5 points cannot establish a predictive relationship"。D138 的 "decreases monotonically" 措辞是对的，但 reviewer 可能要求更多 data points 或 formal model。**防御**：强调这是 observational pattern 而非 predictive model；p=0.0083 的统计意义；dual-variable qualitative mechanism 不依赖 regression。

**Attack 2 (高风险): Cross-model extraction 全用同一个 Mistral-7B**
所有 frontier 模型的 cross-model extraction 都用 Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3。Reviewer 会问：观察到的 pattern 是 "Process-κ 影响 cross-model effectiveness" 还是 "Mistral-7B 对某些 trace 风格的 extraction 能力不同"？即 extractor confound。**防御**：需要 acknowledge 这一点；L1 NULL 结果（Qwen3→Mistral ≈ Mistral→Mistral）部分说明 extractor identity 影响有限。

**Attack 3 (中高风险): Gemini interim 数据 + 无 error bars**
95/204 = 47% 覆盖率。reviewer 会质疑 interim results 的稳定性。且 5-model gradient 中每个点没有 confidence interval。**防御**：bootstrap CI on Δ；标注 interim；FOLIO 不按难度排序的论证。

**3 天准备建议**：
1. 为 Process-κ gradient 的每个点计算 Δ 的 bootstrap 95% CI，加到 scatter plot 上
2. 准备 "extractor confound" 的 rebuttal 段落
3. 如果 westd-25068 恢复，优先完成 Gemini 全量 — 这是消除 Attack 3 的唯一办法

_source line 1110 · 2026-05-21T21:43:38.176Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is a very interesting and important result. Let me analyze it carefully.

The 2×2 matrix shows:

|  | Mistral ext | Qwen3 ext |
|---|---|---|
| Mistral gen (SC=40.2%) | SICA 34.31%, Δ-5.88pp | SICA 79.90%, Δ+39.71pp |
| Qwen3 gen (SC=86.8%) | SICA 37.75%, Δ-49.02pp | SICA 79.90%, Δ-6.86pp |


Now I'm checking whether these results make sense. The self-extraction case with Mistral shows a negative delta, which tracks with the Type C regime where the traces are mostly incorrect and constraints act as noise. The cross-extraction with Qwen3 extractors on Mistral-generated traces shows a dramatic positive jump, suggesting the Qwen3 extractor is much more reliable at recovering the actual constraints despite the poor quality of the input traces. , essentially re-reasoning the problem using its own knowledge rather than truly extracting from the trace. This hypothesis is supported by the fact that SICA performs similarly around 79.9% regardless of whether Mistral or Qwen3 generates the traces — the extractor's capability matters far more than the generator's quality.

But then the cross-model case flips everything: when Mistral tries to extract constraints from Qwen3's correct traces, it crashes to 37.75% accuracy, suggesting Mistral simply can't handle the extraction task effectively even with better input material.

Looking at Qwen3 extracting from its own traces, the self-consistency score stays high at 86.8% but SICA drops to 79.9%, showing confirmation bias at work — the model is confident but not necessarily accurate. The pattern is unmistakable: extractor capability is the bottleneck for SICA performance, not the quality of the traces being extracted from. This fundamentally changes how I should interpret the paper's findings.

The Process-κ gradient story falls apart because the 2×2 matrix shows extractor ability matters far more than trace diversity. And the cross-model extraction results are puzzling — Qwen3 gains 39.71pp when extracting from Mistral traces, while Mistral loses 49pp extracting from Qwen3 traces. This massive asymmetry suggests Qwen3 isn't really extracting constraints from the traces at all, but rather doing independent reasoning that happens to work well on ProofWriter.

Looking at the exact SICA values, Qwen3 achieves 79.90% whether it's reading Mistral's traces or its own — they're identical. That's a smoking gun that Qwen3's extraction is completely independent of the input traces. This contradicts the paper's claim that traces are the bottleneck, at least for smaller models on ProofWriter. The pattern differs sharply from FOLIO where frontier models showed L1≈L0, suggesting the bottleneck dynamics shift depending on model scale and dataset.

Now I'm checking the BR values to see if there are any data integrity issues that might explain these patterns.

Looking at Qwen3's self-extraction results, the BR∞ value of 63.4% seems contradictory at first — that would suggest the gold answer gets zero weight on most problems, yet SICA accuracy is still 79.9%. But BR is only computed on disagreement cases, measuring the constraint weight ratio between the model's preferred answer and gold. Let me reconsider what BR∞ actually represents here. I need to figure out whether those BR∞ percentages apply to all 204 problems or just the ones SICA got wrong. If it's 63.4% of the 41 wrong problems, that's only about 26 cases, but if it's 63.4% of all 204, that's 129 problems — a big difference in interpretation. Let me check the metrics file directly to settle this. On a separate note, Process-κ makes sense as a generator property since it measures trace diversity, and the experiment consistently uses 204 problems across all cells. The real question I need to focus on is understanding that +39.71pp improvement claim.
```

_source line 1114 · 2026-05-21T21:44:27.591Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the full metrics. Let me analyze carefully:

**Key data points:**

| Cell | SC% | SICA% | Δpp | n_wrong | n_inf | BR_mean |
|------|-----|-------|-----|---------|-------|---------|
| Mistral→Mistral (self) | 40.2 | 34.31 | -5.88 | 134 | 43 | 3.44 |
| Mistral→Qwen3 (cross) | 40.2 | 79.90 | +39.71 | 41 | 7 | 2.85 |


| Qwen3→Mistral (cross) | 86.76 | 37.75 | -49.02 | 127 | 58 | 2.16 |
| Qwen3→Qwen3 (self) | 86.76 | 79.90 | -6.86 | 41 | 26 | 1.16 |

The pattern is striking: Qwen3 extractions consistently achieve 79.90% SICA accuracy regardless of the source model, while Mistral extractions drop to around 34-38%. This suggests the extraction quality is fundamentally tied to which model performs the extraction, not where the traces originated. what you'd expect if Qwen3 ignores the traces and reasons independently.

2. **n_wrong is identical for both Qwen3 extractions**: 41 problems wrong in both cases. This suggests the SAME 41 problems are wrong — Qwen3 makes the same errors regardless of whose traces it reads.

3. **SICA accuracy for Mistral extractions**: 34.31% (from Mistral traces) vs 37.75% (from Qwen3 traces). Close but not identical. Slightly better when extracting from Qwen3's (better) traces, but the improvement is tiny compared to the SC gap (40.2% vs 86.76%).

4. **BR patterns**: Mistral's self-extraction shows a BR of 3.44 with 32.1% infinite BR cases, while cross-model extractions vary significantly — Mistral reading Qwen3 traces drops to BR=2.85 with only 17.1% infinite cases, but Qwen3 reading Mistral traces jumps to BR=2.16 with 45.7% infinite cases, and Qwen3's self-extraction is lowest at BR=1.16 with 63.4% infinite cases. These infinite BR percentages represent the proportion of SICA-wrong problems in each scenario.

The data shows strong internal consistency: SC percentage depends solely on which model generates the response, process-κ follows the same pattern, and SICA accuracy is primarily driven by the generator's capability.

This finding directly challenges the L1≈L0 result from earlier work. The 2×2 matrix reveals that when there's a substantial capability gap between models, the stronger extractor essentially performs independent reasoning rather than faithfully extracting from the weaker model's traces. The L1 study showed cross-model extraction performing similarly to same-model extraction with Qwen3→Mistral on FOLIO, but that involved Qwen3-14B extracting from Mistral traces — a different direction and potentially a different dataset effect. The key insight is that Qwen3 excels at extracting formal logic constraints on ProofWriter specifically—the 79.9% result with Mistral traces suggests the constraint extraction quality matters far more than the trace quality itself on this formal reasoning task, whereas on FOLIO the performance gains are minimal regardless of which model extracts constraints. So the constraints from Qwen3's own extraction hurt performance by about 7 percentage points, while extracting from Mistral traces keeps it around 80% — suggesting the constraints do incorporate some trace information, but the MaxSAT aggregation introduces degradation that prevents Qwen3 from reaching its full solo reasoning capability of 87%.

The key insight is that Qwen3's constraint extraction ability itself appears to be the limiting factor, not the quality of the input traces. Whether extracting from its own traces or Mistral's, the SICA performance plateaus around 80%, which points to a ceiling on how well Qwen3 can generate correct formal logic constraints — something fundamentally different from its raw reasoning accuracy.

This actually reframes the paper's narrative: extractor capability matters far more than trace quality, which partially contradicts the assumption that traces are the bottleneck. The extractor's own constraint-generation ability is what's constraining performance, not the input it's working from.

But there's a puzzle here — the FOLIO and ProofWriter results diverge dramatically despite this extractor effect, and I need to figure out why. ProofWriter being synthetic auto-generated data might explain why Qwen3 performs better there, possibly due to training data overlap, whereas FOLIO's human-authored complexity creates a different challenge. This discrepancy could undermine the trace bottleneck claim if I don't properly account for dataset differences.

The real test is whether those 41 wrong problems are identical across both Qwen3 extraction conditions — if they are, that's strong evidence the extractor is essentially re-solving the problem independently of the traces, which would mean "extraction" is misleading terminology.
```

_source line 1123 · 2026-05-21T21:46:25.075Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   I am operating as the Research Reviewer (独立科学审查员) for project `symb_invariant_consensus` in a continuous monitoring loop. My role is to independently verify experiment conclusions, check GPU whitelist compliance, audit data consistency, and report findings to the Director via `reviewer_post_to_director`. The project studies "Symbolic Mirage" — how self-extracted logical constraints project model bias — targeting EMNLP 2026 ARR submission on May 25, 2026. All output must be in Chinese. Key tasks this session:
   - Monitor all running experiments on westd-16639 and westd-25068
   - Verify the extraction_qwen3_same backup restore
   - Pre-assess V16 paper weak points before formal review
   - Audit the cross-model SICA 2×2 harvest results (most recent task)

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **SCB (Symbolic Confirmation Bias)**: Self-extracted constraints project model bias (BR>1)
   - **SICA**: Symbolic Invariant Constraint Aggregation — the paper's method using MaxSAT over FOL constraints
   - **SC**: Self-Consistency (majority vote baseline)
   - **BR (Bias Ratio)**: Measures constraint bias toward model's own answer. BR=∞ means gold answer gets 0 weight.
   - **Process-κ**: TF-IDF based kappa measuring trace diversity across CoT reasoning paths
   - **Answer Fleiss' κ**: Inter-trace answer agreement
   - **Cross-model extraction**: Using Model A to extract FOL constraints from Model B's traces
   - **2×2 cross-model matrix**: Generator(row) × Extractor(col) design with Mistral-7B and Qwen3-14B on ProofWriter
   - **Dual-variable model (D137)**: Cross-model extraction gain = f(trace diversity [low κ], bias severity [high BR])
   - **Spearman ρ**: Rank correlation. n=5: ρ=-1.0, p=0.0083 (one-tailed) for Process-κ → Δ gradient
   - **Quality Ladder**: L0 (self) ≈ L1 (cross-model) << L3 (oracle) on FOLIO — traces are bottleneck
   - **Extractor confound**: All frontier cross-model extraction uses single Mistral-7B as extractor

8. Current Work:
   I had just read and analyzed the `crossmodel_2x2_metrics.json` from the 2×2 harvest worker. The results show a dramatic pattern:

   | | Mistral ext | Qwen3 ext |
   |---|---|---|
   | Mistral gen (SC=40.2%) | SICA 34.31%, Δ-5.88pp | SICA 79.90%, Δ+39.71pp |
   | Qwen3 gen (SC=86.8%) | SICA 37.75%, Δ-49.02pp | SICA 79.90%, Δ-6.86pp |

   Critical findings I was preparing to report:
   1. **SICA accuracy identical (79.90%) for BOTH Qwen3 extraction cells** — regardless of whether traces come from Mistral (SC=40.2%) or Qwen3 (SC=86.76%). Strong evidence Qwen3 reasons independently of input traces.
   2. **n_wrong identical (41) for both Qwen3 cells** — likely the SAME 41 problems fail.
   3. **Extractor capability completely dominates**: ~34-38% for Mistral ext, ~79.9% for Qwen3 ext.
   4. **Potential contradiction with L1≈L0 Quality Ladder on FOLIO**: On FOLIO, Qwen3 extracting from Mistral traces ≈ Mistral self-extraction (60.29% vs 59.31%). On ProofWriter, the gap is massive (79.9% vs 34.31%).
   5. **Implication for paper narrative**: The "traces are the bottleneck" claim (supported by L1≈L0 on FOLIO) may be dataset-dependent, not universal. On ProofWriter, the extractor is the bottleneck.

   I was about to compose and post a detailed `reviewer_post_to_director` finding about these 2×2 results.

```json
{
  "text": "[severity: warning] 2×2 Cross-Model SICA 结果审查 — Extractor 主导效应与 L1≈L0 矛盾\n\n## 核心发现\n\ncrossmodel_2x2_metrics.json 数据揭示一个重要模式：**Extractor 能力完全主导 SICA 准确率，Generator traces 几乎无影响**。\n\n### 证据\n\n| Generator → Extractor | SC | SICA | Δpp | n_wrong |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| Mistral → Mistral | 40.2% | 34.31% | -5.88 | 134 |\n| Mistral → **Qwen3** | 40.2% | **79.90%** | +39.71 | **41** |\n| Qwen3 → Mistral | 86.8% | 37.75% | -49.02 | 127 |\n| Qwen3 → **Qwen3** | 86.8% | **79.90%** | -6.86 | **41** |\n\n**关键数据点：**\n1. Qwen3 作为 extractor 的两个 cell SICA **完全相同**（79.90%），n_wrong **完全相同**（41）——极大概率是同一批 41 题失败。这意味着 Qwen3 提取约束时**几乎完全忽略输入 traces 的内容**，独立推理得出结论。\n2. Mistral 作为 extractor 无论收到谁的 traces 都在 34-38%，同样说明 extractor 自身能力是瓶颈。\n3. +39.71pp 的\"提升\"本质上不是 cross-model extraction 的功劳——是 Qwen3 用自身推理能力\"覆盖\"了 Mistral 的弱 traces。\n\n## 与 FOLIO Quality Ladder 的矛盾\n\npaper_framing.md 的 Quality Ladder（FOLIO 数据）支撑\"L1≈L0 → traces are the bottleneck\"：\n- FOLIO: Qwen3 extracting Mistral traces (60.29%) ≈ Mistral self-extraction (59.31%)\n\n但 ProofWriter 2×2 数据讲的是**完全相反的故事**：\n- ProofWriter: Qwen3 extracting Mistral traces (79.90%) >> Mistral self-extraction (34.31%)\n\n**这说明\"traces are the bottleneck\" vs \"extractor is the bottleneck\" 是 dataset-dependent 的，不是 universal claim。** FOLIO 上 traces 是瓶颈（可能因为 FOLIO 的自然语言推理更难，traces 质量真的很差），ProofWriter 上 extractor 是瓶颈（可能因为 ProofWriter 的 symbolic 结构更明确，强 extractor 能绕过弱 traces）。\n\n## 对论文叙事的影响\n\n1. **Claim 11（SCB universality）**：2×2 数据可以支撑\"SCB exists cross-model\"（BR>1 在所有 cell），但具体模式因数据集而异。\n2. **Claim 12（Process-κ gradient）**：2×2 的 κ 只取决于 generator（Mistral 0.619, Qwen3 0.836），与 5-model frontier gradient 是不同维度的分析，需要谨慎整合。\n3. **Qwen3 的 79.90% 不是\"cross-model extraction gain\"**：是 Qwen3 自身能力的体现。如果论文把 Mistral→Qwen3 的 +39.71pp 包装成\"cross-model debiasing works\"，审稿人只需指出 Qwen3 self SICA 也是 79.90% 就能击溃这个 claim。\n\n## 建议\n\n1. **整合策略**：2×2 数据作为 SCB 机制分析（BR/κ 与 generator 绑定）是安全的，但**不要用它 claim \"cross-model extraction improves accuracy\"**——改为 claim \"extractor capability determines ceiling, generator traces determine floor\"。\n2. **叙事一致性**：需要明确区分 FOLIO（trace-bottleneck regime）和 ProofWriter（extractor-bottleneck regime），这反而可以成为一个更细致的贡献——**regime-dependent bottleneck analysis**。\n3. **风险**：如果 V16 patch 简单地把 +39.71pp 作为 cross-model 的成功案例，审稿人会直接质疑\"这只是换了个更强的模型做推理而已\"。",
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The pipeline has fully completed. Let me analyze the final results:

**Full 2×2 Pipeline Results (N=204, ProofWriter D5):**

| Condition | Acc | N |
|---|---|---|
| Mistral SC | 0.4020 | 82 |
| Qwen3 SC | 0.8676 | 177 |
| mistral_same | 0.3431 | 204 |
| mistral_cross_qwen3 | 0.7990 | 204 |


| qwen3_same | 0.7990 | 204 |
| qwen3_cross_mistral | 0.3775 | 204 |

Now I'm looking at the delta percentage point comparisons between conditions. There's something odd here — the cross-model extraction for Mistral shows a negative delta compared to its single-choice baseline, but the raw numbers suggest a much larger positive difference. I need to figure out what these delta calculations are actually measuring, since they don't seem to align with the straightforward accuracy differences I'm seeing in the table. I'm trying to figure out what "mistral cross" actually refers to in the naming convention. Looking at the specific numbers, if it means cross-model extraction applied to Mistral traces, then the deltas don't line up with what I'm calculating. Let me reconsider whether the naming might indicate something different about which model is the generator versus the extractor. the "mistral cross" value of 0.3775 matches the difference between Mistral extracting from Qwen3 traces and the Mistral SC baseline, so that's the right interpretation. Now I'm verifying the cross-model extraction deltas for Qwen3 — when it extracts from Mistral traces it drops to 79.90% versus its self-contained 86.76%, a -6.86pp gap, but interestingly when comparing against its same-model SICA baseline it stays flat at 79.90%. The naming pattern appears to follow "{model}

_{extraction_type}" where "cross" indicates the model is doing extraction across different model traces. Looking at the bias ratio data, I'm noticing these values (0.6664, 0.8088, etc.) seem to represent the fraction of wrong answers containing bias rather than the mean bias ratio from the earlier harvest report, which had much larger numbers like 3.4424 and 2.8508. The pipeline's final output is computing a different BR metric than the harvest worker's arithmetic mean — possibly something like accuracy-weighted BR or the fraction of constraints supporting the modal answer. This distinction doesn't really matter for my audit since the key numbers still line up.

The McNemar tests show no significant differences: Mistral's self-extraction versus cross-extraction from Qwen3 traces yields p=0.40, and Qwen3's self-extraction versus cross-extraction from Mistral traces yields p=0.86. Both results suggest the extraction source doesn't meaningfully affect performance.

But wait — I'm second-guessing the naming convention here. When I see "mistral_same_vs_cross" with a p-value of 0.40, I'd expect a massive difference between Mistral's self-extraction at 34.31% and its cross-extraction at 79.90%, yet McNemar shows no significance. The "12 vs 12" in the test refers to discordant pairs — 12 problems where one method succeeded and the other failed, balanced equally in both directions — which seems odd given those percentages.

Actually, I think I've been misreading this. The comparison is likely between the same extractor with different generator traces: Mistral extracting from Mistral-generated traces versus Mistral extracting from Qwen3-generated traces. That small 3.43 percentage point difference with balanced discordant pairs explains the p=0.40 result. For Qwen3, the extractors show identical performance regardless of trace source, giving p=0.86. This strongly suggests that extractors work independently of where the input traces come from.

Now looking at the Fleiss kappa values across different conditions: Mistral shows very low agreement at 0.1853, indicating its extracted constraints vary significantly across traces, while Qwen3 achieves substantial agreement at 0.7615, and cross-model extraction lands in the middle at 0.3135. The pipeline ran successfully across all 204 samples, completing the full analysis.

The McNemar test results with p-values of 0.40 and 0.86 are striking—they indicate the extractors are producing essentially identical outputs regardless of which generator's traces they're analyzing. This strongly suggests the extractors aren't actually reading the trace content but instead applying some consistent internal logic. I should flag this finding with the Director and then verify the complete results file to ensure all the data is there.
```

```text
Now I have the complete results.json. Let me analyze the key findings:

**results.json full data:**

Config: N=204, K=12, T_gen=0.7, T_ext=0.3, seed=42

**SICA accuracy:**
| Condition | Correct | Total | Acc |
|---|---|---|---|
| mistral_same | 70 | 204 | 34.31% |
| mistral_cross_qwen3 | 163 | 204 | 79.90% |


| qwen3_same | 163 | 204 | 79.90% |
| qwen3_cross_mistral | 77 | 204 | 37.75% |

The most striking pattern here is that mistral_cross_qwen3 and qwen3_same achieved identical accuracy with exactly 163 correct answers—they almost certainly got the same questions right. Looking at the constraint statistics, I'm seeing major differences in how many constraints each condition generated and how many were unique.

Generating markdown table... Qwen3 extracts fewer but higher-quality constraints when analyzing its own traces, yet produces significantly more constraints when processing Mistral traces—suggesting it reasons independently rather than simply relying on the input trace verbosity. The McNemar test comparing Mistral self-extraction versus Qwen3 cross-extraction shows a modest improvement of 3.44 percentage points, with 15 problems solved by the cross approach that Mistral's self-extraction missed. So the delta_pp values are organized by which model is doing the extracting: Mistral shows a +3.43pp improvement when extracting from Qwen3 traces versus its own, while Qwen3 shows no difference at 0.00pp whether extracting from its own traces or Mistral's. The McNemar tests confirm this same structure—comparing each extractor's self-extraction against its cross-extraction performance.

Looking at the bias ratio data, these values are clearly different from the harvest metrics, suggesting they're measuring something distinct—possibly a different calculation or proportion rather than the mean ratio of constraint weights. What's striking is that Qwen3's bias ratio drops noticeably when extracting from Mistral traces instead of its own, falling from 0.8088 to 0.6500, a meaningful decrease of 0.1588.

Yet this reduction in bias doesn't translate to any accuracy improvement—it stays at 79.90%. This suggests the extractor's reasoning ability sets the accuracy ceiling, independent of bias levels, which is an important distinction for the narrative.

The Fleiss kappa values reveal interesting consistency patterns: Mistral traces show only slight agreement at 0.1853, while Qwen3 traces demonstrate substantial agreement at 0.7615, with cross-model agreement falling in between at 0.3135. These align with the Process-κ findings but measure constraint agreement rather than trace-level similarity.

The pipeline is complete with all 204 samples across conditions, and the results are clear: extractor identity entirely determines SICA accuracy, McNemar tests show no significant effect from trace source, and while constraint counts vary widely, accuracy remains constant per extractor. Cross-model extraction slightly reduces Qwen3's bias but leaves accuracy unchanged, and bias is present universally across all conditions.

I should highlight the McNemar statistics as the strongest evidence for this extractor-dominance pattern. Let me verify the overlap between Qwen3's same-source and cross-model conditions—if both achieve 163 correct answers with 12 discordant cases in each direction, that means 151 questions are answered correctly in both, with 12 unique to each condition, totaling 175 correct across the union.

Actually, let me recalculate: 151 overlap both correct, 12 same-only correct, 12 cross-only correct, and 29 both wrong—that's 204 total questions. So 74% of questions produce identical outcomes regardless of trace source, while only 12% flip either direction. The symmetrical 12-12 split suggests these discrepancies are essentially random noise rather than systematic trace-quality effects. I'll focus my follow-up on the McNemar test and constraint count evidence as the most compelling support.
```

```json
  "text": "[severity: warning] 2×2 审查补充 — McNemar 统计检验确认 Extractor 独立推理\n\nPipeline 完整输出（results.json）提供了关键统计证据，补充前一条审查。\n\n## McNemar 检验（同一 extractor, 不同 generator traces）\n\n| Extractor | Same Acc | Cross Acc | Δpp | same_only | cross_only | p |\n|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| Mistral | 34.31% | 37.75% | +3.44 | 8 | 15 | 0.400 |\n| Qwen3 | 79.90% | 79.90% | 0.00 | 12 | 12 | 0.862 |\n\n**两个 p 值都远大于 0.05**——对于任一 extractor，更换 generator traces 对准确率没有统计显著影响。\n\nQwen3 的 12 vs 12 discordant pairs 意味着：204 题中有 151 题两种条件给出相同答案（74%），只有 24 题（12%）翻转——且翻转方向完美对称（各 12 题），属于随机噪声而非系统性的 trace 质量效应。\n\n## 约束数量差异（SICA 准确率相同）\n\n| Condition | avg_constraints | avg_unique | Accuracy |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| qwen3_same | 37.89 | 17.85 | 79.90% |\n| mistral_cross_qwen3 | 96.32 | 33.50 | 79.90% |\n\nQwen3 从 Mistral traces 提取的约束数量是自提取的 **2.5 倍**（96 vs 38），但准确率**完全相同**。这进一步证明 Qwen3 的 SICA 准确率由其推理能力决定，与约束数量/来源无关。\n\n## Bias Ratio 降低但不影响准确率\n\n- Qwen3 same BR=0.8088 → cross BR=0.6500, **Δ=-0.16**\n- Cross-model extraction 确实降低了 Qwen3 的 bias ratio，但这对准确率**零影响**（都是 79.90%）\n\n## 对论文的建议\n\n这组数据可以支撑的安全 claim：\n1. **SCB 跨模型存在**（所有 cell BR>0.5）✓\n2. **Cross-model extraction 可降低 BR**（Qwen3 Δ=-0.16）✓\n3. **Extractor 能力决定 SICA 天花板**（McNemar 证据）✓\n\n不应 claim 的：\n- \"Cross-model extraction improves accuracy\"——McNemar 不显著\n- \"Trace diversity helps\"——约束数量 2.5x 差异不改变结果\n\n建议将 ProofWriter 2×2 定位为 **SCB mechanism analysis**（BR 行为 + extractor ceiling effect），而非 accuracy improvement 实验。",
  "project": "symb_invariant_consensus"
}
```

```json
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",
```

```text
The worker's status check aligns with what I already found and reported. Let me verify the key data points:

2. **Qwen3→Mistral fix**: 100% complete ✅ — was 87/152, now done
3. **Gemini cross-model extraction**: 95/95 complete ✅ — but wait, this is only 95 problems (the interim data). The full 204 Gemini SC traces haven't been completed yet.

Wait, the worker reports "Gemini cross-model extraction: 95/95, 100% complete, Cross-model SICA=86.3%, Original=87.4%, SC=87.4%"


So the Gemini extraction on the available 95 SC traces is complete, but the full 204 Gemini SC is still pending on westd-25068. I'm also noticing a discrepancy in the cross-model SICA table — the worker shows "Mistral cross-SICA (by Qwen3): 37.7%", but checking the results.json directly, mistral_cross_qwen3 is actually 79.90% while qwen3_cross_mistral is 37.75%, so the labels appear to be swapped. I'm looking at the results.json data to clarify the naming convention — the delta_pp section is organized by extractor, not generator, so "Mistral cross-SICA (by Qwen3)" at 37.7% means Mistral extracting from Qwen3 traces, while "Qwen3 cross-SICA (by Mistral)" at 79.9% means Qwen3 extracting from Mistral traces. be mistral_cross_qwen3 = 79.90%, not 37.7%. But if I interpret "by Qwen3" as Qwen3 being the extractor working from Mistral traces, then it maps to qwen3_cross_mistral = 37.75%, which matches. So the worker's labeling is just inverted from what I'd expect — the percentages themselves line up with the actual data.

Looking at the full pattern now: "X cross-SICA (by Y)" actually means X as the extractor pulling from Y as the generator, which is the cross-model extraction setup. Mistral extracting from Qwen3 gives 37.7%, and Qwen3 extracting from Mistral gives 79.9%, both of which are correct values from the results. So the pattern is "{Extractor} cross-SICA (by {Generator})" where the extractor works on traces from the generator. Now I'm seeing the interim Gemini results across all three metrics—SICA at 86.3%, Original at 87.4%, and SC also at 87.4%—all computed on the full 95 interim data points.

The cross-model SICA is running about 1.1 percentage points below SC, which aligns with my earlier interim findings. The worker's report confirms what I already have, so my three reports cover the necessary ground. I'm still waiting on the westd-25068 recovery and the Paper Agent V16 Patch expected later today.

Now I'm checking on a couple of other experiments that need attention — the exp-d126-70b-sica registry cleanup and getting Gemini's full 204 SC completion wrapped up.
```

```text
Now I'm noticing some inconsistencies that need clarification. The ROADMAP indicates V16 is submitted for review, but the status says "pending user approval review mode" — this appears to be an internal review cycle rather than the actual external submission. I should verify what stage we're actually at. Also, the Process-κ gradient parameters claim n=5 with p=0.0083, but I need to double-check whether these values are accurate or if there's been a recent update.

The Gemini data is still interim at 95 out of 204 changes, which means the Process-κ estimate could shift once the full dataset is available. While 95 points should give a reasonably stable interim value around 0.790, relying on incomplete data for the main statistical claim introduces risk. The D140 framing decision to move the 2×2 extractor dominance data to the appendix makes sense since that's mechanism analysis rather than a core accuracy finding. The bootstrap CI approach for Process-κ should help address the uncertainty here.

The timeline is compressed but workable: westd-25068 needs to recover within 24 hours, V16 review should surface no blockers, and the full Gemini dataset shouldn't dramatically alter the gradient. There are still some registry issues to clean up, including a process that's marked as failed but still running.

Looking at what's missing from the roadmap, the McNemar p-values and constraint count asymmetry should probably be in the appendix, and I need to verify whether V16 currently flags the interim Gemini data appropriately. The roadmap itself is mostly solid—it correctly identifies westd-25068 as the critical blocker and scopes D140 to appendix work—but there's a tension between claiming statistical significance with n=5 interim data that hasn't been fully disclosed.

The registry also has stale entries that need cleaning up, and I'm concerned about how the extractor-dominance finding in the appendix might interact with the main narrative about cross-model extraction. The roadmap is reasonable overall, so I should post a focused review highlighting these remaining risks rather than raising broad objections.
```

_source line 1246 · 2026-05-21T22:02:24.495Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me analyze the Bootstrap CI results:

**GPU Cleanup:**
- GPU 0 and 1 freed (~168 GB total)
- GPU 3 kept (Mistral:8004 for Gemini extraction)
- ModelScope stuck downloads killed
- This is exactly what I recommended. Good.

**Bootstrap CI:**
- ρ = -1.000, 95% CI = [-1.000, -1.000]
- All 9,982 valid bootstrap samples have ρ = -1.000
- 18/10,000 samples were degenerate (constant input → NaN)


- This is problematic — with only 5 data points where κ values are strictly monotonic with Δ, nearly every resample preserves enough distinct points to yield perfect correlation, making the CI uninformative about actual uncertainty in the relationship. are [+2.45, +0.49, 0.00, -1.05, -1.47], these are:
- κ strictly increasing: 0.248 < 0.563 < 0.596 < 0.790 < 0.792
- Δ strictly decreasing: +2.45 > +0.49 > 0.00 > -1.05 > -1.47

So yes, Spearman ρ = -1.0 exactly. Bootstrap resamples will mostly preserve this perfect rank correlation, though near-ties in the original data like κ=0.790 and κ=0.792 could theoretically swap ordering in some resamples. The real issue is that a bootstrap CI on Spearman ρ with n=5 and perfect correlation is methodologically problematic—the actual uncertainty should come from the p-value (p=0.0083 is respectable for n=5) and the measurement error in the underlying κ and Δ values themselves.

The bootstrap treats each (κ, Δ) pair as fixed, so it misses the measurement uncertainty within each model's K=12 traces. To properly quantify uncertainty, I'd need to bootstrap the κ and Δ values separately from the raw traces, then check how often the gradient relationship holds. A CI of [-1.000, -1.000] will look degenerate and suspicious to reviewers—they'll recognize this as a sign the sample size is too small for meaningful bootstrap inference, and that the permutation p-value is the more appropriate test statistic here. I should definitely flag this methodological concern.

The bootstrap CI would be misleading if included in the paper, and a reviewer would likely criticize it as a misapplication of bootstrap methods. The permutation test with p=0.0083 is actually the stronger evidence. I need to verify the bootstrap implementation by checking the script on the server.
```
